### *Basil: in the Hexameron, Book 9.* <note>St. Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD) was an influential theologian; the *Hexameron* is his series of sermons on the six days of creation.</note>

If someone immerses their soul into every single word of the Psalms,
just as the sense of taste is applied to discern the flavor of food: 
It is they who fulfill what is written: *Sing praises with understanding.* <note>original Latin: "Psallite sapienter," a reference to Psalm 47:7.</note>

They truly love God who occupy themselves with nothing 
other than that by which they may glorify His Name.

### *Ambrose: Book on the Instruction of a*
### *Virgin, Chapter 2.* <note>St. Ambrose (c. 339–397 AD) was the Bishop of Milan; this work is a treatise on the virtues of the religious life.</note>

A prayer is good when it follows the proper order—that 
we begin first with divine praises. For if, 
when we deal with human men, we seek to make a 
judge well-disposed toward us, how much more should we do so 
when we pray to our Lord?

# PREFACE.

<sig>* 2</sig>

# TO THE ALMIGHTY KING AND MOST HOLY GOD

## TO JEHOVAH <note>original: "Iehovae," the Latinization of the Hebrew name for God, often used in this period to emphasize the biblical and eternal nature of the Creator.</note>, THE LORD OF GODS, THE MIGHTIEST LORD OF LORDS, the greatest Creator of heaven and earth, and their perpetual Ruler and Preserver, his most Merciful Patron and most generous Benefactor. <note>original: "Mecoenati," a reference to Gaius Maecenas, a famous Roman patron of the arts; here used to describe God as the ultimate provider.</note>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut initial letter 'O' set within a square frame, featuring intricate Renaissance-style scrollwork with vine and leaf motifs.</image-desc>

Almighty eternal **GOD**, the generosity and blessings bestowed from Your divine Majesty, through Your singular mercy and kindness, upon me, a miserable sinner, are so immense and manifold that I could never return the due and deserved gratitude to You for them. Nevertheless, lest I should appear altogether ungrateful in this matter, being mindful of the favors I have received, I have often deliberated with myself by what means I might, in some small way, show myself grateful to **Your Divine Majesty** <note>original: "D. Maiest. Tuam," where 'D' stands for 'Divinae' (Divine).</note> in both deed and spirit. Since, therefore, during this winter...

<summary>This page begins the formal preface of the book, presented as a grand dedication to God. The author expresses overwhelming gratitude for divine blessings and explains his desire to offer this work as a token of his thankfulness.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Jehovah, Divine Majesty, gratitude, Creator, Providence, Preface, early modern piety</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Praefatio" (Preface) addressed to God. The previous page ended with "Since, therefore, during this winter..." and this page completes the thought, describing the author's metaphorical walk through a divine garden to gather the "flowers" that make up this book.</meta>

...time, for the sake of refreshing my soul, I was walking in **Your Paradise**, which is most beautifully adorned with all kinds of seeds, trees, flowers, and plants. I marveled at its varied and delightful beauty as well as its great abundance, and while walking, with the permission of **Your Gardener** <note>original: "Hortulani," likely referring to Christ or the Holy Spirit who oversees the spiritual "garden" of the scriptures or the world.</note>, I plucked and gathered various beautiful little flowers, which were also most excellent in the sweetness of their fragrance. For a long time, I was occupied with anxious care and pondered in my mind what I might compose from them that would be pleasing and acceptable to **Your Divine Majesty**.

Since, however, I considered that it was not granted to my human weakness that I should be able (as **bees** do) to craft a very sweet honey from those little flowers, I wished—prompted by Your Gardener—to weave together some *Paucarpias* <note>original: "Paucarpias," a term derived from Greek meaning a collection of various fruits or an offering of mixed produce.</note>, or little garlands, which I humbly offer to **Your Divine Majesty** as my **first fruits** <note>original: "primitias," a biblical term for the first part of a harvest dedicated to God.</note>. I do so trusting that their most fragrant scent (especially because these plants [are] eternal...)

->TO THE READER.<-

# TO THE READER.

That the science which is called KABBALAH, or the Cabalistic Art, is the highest and by far the most excellent of all other sciences, we have most clearly demonstrated elsewhere in a specific Treatise, etc., from the very fountains of the sacred scriptures. It remains that here, in place of a preface, we should say something beforehand concerning the title of this little book, and assign the reasons why we have entitled these prayers "Cabalistic."

Now, **KABBALAH** (so-called from the Hebrew word *Kibel* <note>original: "Kibel". From the Hebrew root *QBL* (קבל), meaning "to receive."</note>, which means "he received" or "he accepted") is a teaching that is not implanted in our minds by nature, nor propagated along with the seeds from our parents, nor observed by use or experience, nor extracted from nature, nor drawn from books composed by human wisdom. Instead, it is divinely promulgated, and as if infused, sent into, and revealed to the souls of holy Men by **GOD** himself, and commended and confirmed by heavenly voices and testimonies. Such are most of the writings contained in the books of both the New and the Old <margin>Old</margin> Testament: For Saint Paul testifies that that scripture is divinely inspired; and concerning the same, the Apostle Peter reports, that it was not brought by human will, but holy Men spoke, being inspired by the Holy Spirit. For it is certain from

<summary>The author introduces the reader to the concept of Kabbalah, defining it not as human philosophy but as divine revelation "received" from God. He justifies titling his collection of prayers "Cabalistic" by linking the term to the divinely inspired nature of the Holy Scriptures.</summary>

<keywords>Kabbalah, Cabalistic Art, Kibel, revelation, divine inspiration, Saint Paul, Saint Peter, Old Testament, New Testament</keywords>

# TO THE READER.

the **Old Testament**, that **Moses** received the <term>Law of the Decalogue</term><note>The Ten Commandments.</note> from **God** himself. In this way, **God** spoke through the mouths of the **Prophets**. Even the **Royal Psalmist** <note>King David, traditionally credited as the author of the Psalms.</note> says in his most famous words: *The Spirit of the Lord has spoken through me, and his word is upon my tongue.* And elsewhere he says: *The Lord has put a new song into my mouth, a hymn to our God.*

In the **New Testament**, **Christ** himself says to his **Apostles**: *The words that I speak to you, I do not speak from myself. But the Father dwelling in me, he performs the works.* And concerning his **Apostles**, he says to the **Father**: *Now they have known that all things which you have given me are from you: Because the words which you gave me, I have given to them,* etc. 

Concerning the **Holy Spirit**, he speaks to the **Apostles** in this way: *But when that Spirit of truth comes, he will teach you all truth: for he will not speak from himself, but whatever he hears, he will speak, and he will announce to you the things that are to come.* Similarly, he addresses his **Apostles**, saying: *For it is not you who speak, but the Spirit of your Father who speaks in you.* 

Likewise, Saint **Paul** does not dare to speak of anything which **Christ** has not brought about through him, both in word and in deed. And regarding his **Gospel**, which was preached by him, he says that it is not according to man, since he did not receive or learn it from Man, but through the revelation of **Jesus Christ**. He also rebukes those who do not glorify **God** in the proper way, saying: *They are without excuse, because when they had known God, they did not glorify*—

# TO THE READER.

Since these things are so, and since these prayers of ours were not devised by human wit, but were taken from the very divine oracles inspired by **God** himself, and contain nothing other than the pure word of **God**, we have judged it not undeserved to entitle them <term>Cabalistic</term><note>From the Hebrew "Kabbalah," meaning "received tradition." The author uses it here to mean knowledge received directly from divine revelation rather than human invention.</note>. For if we do not know what we should pray for as we ought, and therefore need the **Spirit** to help our weakness, the method of glorifying **God** should be sought from no other source than from that spirit of grace and prayers. This spirit teaches and instructs us through the **Apostle** <note>Saint Paul.</note>, saying: *Whatever you do in word or in deed, do all in the Name of our Lord **Jesus Christ**, giving thanks to **God** and the **Father** through him.* And in the **Song of the sons of Korah** <note>original: "Cantico filiorum Cores"; referring to Psalm 48:10.</note> it is written: *According to your Name, O **God**, so is your praise unto the ends of the earth.* 

Wherefore **God** also promises that in every place where the memory of his **Name** shall be, he will come to us and bless us. From these testimonies it is clear that the method of praising **God** consists chiefly in the glorification and sanctification of the divine **Name**. For greater confirmation of this matter, we see that even **Christ** himself, in his own prayer <note>The Lord's Prayer.</note>, willed to place the sanctification of **God’s** **Name** before the other petitions. Now, what that sanctification or glorification of the divine **Name** might be, the **Psalmist** declares in brief yet powerful words, saying: I will sing—

<summary>The author justifies naming his collection of prayers "Cabalistic," arguing that true prayer must be "received" from divine scripture rather than human invention. He emphasizes that the highest form of worship is the sanctification of God's Name, as evidenced by the Psalms and the Lord's Prayer.</summary>

# TO THE READER.

<sig>* 5</sig>

...[Similarly, we] will sing and sing psalms of your virtues. <note>original: "cantabimus & psallemus virtutes tuas"; this is the conclusion of Psalm 21:14.</note> Likewise: *Praise him in his mighty acts: praise him according to the multitude of his greatness.* <note>Psalm 150:2</note> The names, titles, and adjectives that are variously attributed to **God** by the sacred scriptures were devised for this purpose: to signify the very nature of **God**. Since His nature is unique and simple, it ought to be named with a single name; yet these [various terms] were established so that they might not only explain His infinite virtues and the multitude of His greatness, but also declare the many and varied blessings flowing most abundantly from divine goodness.

We see, therefore, that in the verses contained in the prayers of the Patriarchs, Prophets, Apostles, and other holy men—and especially in the **Psalms of David**—the unspeakable Name of **God**: the <term>Tetragrammaton</term><note>From the Greek for "four letters," referring to the sacred Hebrew name of God, YHWH, which was considered too holy to be spoken aloud.</note> (which we otherwise express as <term>Adonai</term><note>Hebrew for "my Lord," used by readers as a substitute for the Tetragrammaton.</note>), and other divine Names are very frequently included and explained. Through these, when **God** has become known, His virtues reveal themselves, which carry us away into admiration of Him and incite us to celebrate His praise. For it is scarcely possible, once a person has conceived of these virtues of **God**, that they would not immediately fix every intention of their mind and reason upon truly knowing <note>original: "noscendum" (to be known), though the OCR reads "nascendum" (to be born).</note> and loving **God**.

Therefore, through these <term>Cabalistic</term><note>The author uses this term to refer to a tradition of prayer rooted in the revealed names of God rather than human invention.</note> prayers of ours, the highest divinity can be most excellently celebrated and glorified—not indeed by fabricated or new titles, but by those most holy Names of **God** brought forth by the **Holy Spirit** himself and described in the sacred pages:

<summary>The author explains that the various names of God in the Bible, such as the Tetragrammaton, are divine revelations intended to help humans understand and love God's infinite nature. He justifies the title "Cabalistic" for his prayers because they rely on these holy, spirit-inspired names rather than human imagination.</summary>

# TO THE READER.

...[they neither glorified Him] nor gave thanks, but became vain in their thoughts. <note>original: "evanuerunt in cogitationibus suis"; a reference to the Apostle Paul's description of those who turned away from God in Romans 1:21.</note>

Since this is the case, and since these prayers of ours were not devised by human wit, but were taken from the very divine oracles inspired by **God** Himself, and contain nothing other than the pure word of **God**, we have judged it not undeserved to title them <term>Cabalistic</term>. <note>In this 16th or 17th-century context, "Cabalistic" refers to the idea of "received" or "handed down" divine wisdom, specifically prayers constructed directly from the Hebrew scriptures and the names of God.</note> For if we do not know what we should pray for as we ought, and therefore need the **Spirit** to help our infirmity, the method of glorifying **God** should be sought from no other source but that **Spirit** of grace and prayers. This Spirit teaches and instructs us through the **Apostle**, saying: *Whatever you do in word or in deed, do all in the Name of our Lord Jesus Christ, giving thanks to God and the Father through him.* <note>Colossians 3:17</note>

And in the **Song of the Sons of Korah** it is held: *According to your Name, O God, so is your praise unto the ends of the earth.* <note>Psalm 48:10</note> Wherefore **God** also promises that in every place where there is a memorial of His Name, He will come to us and bless us. From these testimonies it is clear that the method of praising **God** consists primarily in the glorification and sanctification of the divine Name. For greater confirmation of this matter, we see that even **Christ** Himself, in His own prayer <note>The Lord's Prayer ("Our Father"), which begins with "Hallowed be Thy Name."</note>, wished to place the sanctification of **God’s** Name before all other petitions.

The **Psalmist** declares the nature of that sanctification or glorification of the divine Name in brief yet vigorous words, saying: We shall sing—

# TO THE READER.

...we will confirm and sing of your virtues. Item: *Praise him in his virtues: praise him according to the multitude of his greatness.* <note>original: "Laudate eum in virtutibus eius: laudate eum secundum multitudinem magnitudinis eius." A quotation from Psalm 150:2.</note> The names, titles, and adjectives which are variously attributed to **God** by the sacred scriptures were devised for this purpose: to signify the very nature of **GOD**. Although His nature is single and simple—and should ideally be named with a single name—these titles were established so that they might not only explain His infinite virtues and the multitude of His greatness, but also declare the many and various goods flowing most abundantly from the divine goodness.

We see, therefore, that in the verses found in the prayers of the Patriarchs, Prophets, Apostles, and other holy men—and especially in the **Davidic Psalms**—the ineffable Name of **GOD**, the <term>Tetragrammaton</term> <note>The "four-letter" name of God in Hebrew (YHWH), considered too sacred to pronounce.</note> (which we otherwise utter as <term>Adonay</term> <note>Hebrew for "Lord," used as a substitute for the sacred four-letter name.</note>), and other divine Names are very often contained and explained. Through these names, once **God** has become known, His virtues put themselves forward; these seize us with admiration for Him and incite us to celebrate His praise. For it is scarcely possible, once a person has conceived of these virtues of **GOD**, that they would not immediately fix every intention of mind and reason upon truly knowing <note>The original Latin reads "nascendum" (to be born), but given the pairing with "amandum" (to be loved), this is almost certainly a printer's error for "noscendum" (to be known).</note> and loving **God**.

Therefore, through these <term>Cabalistic</term> <note>In this context, the author uses "Cabalistic" to mean "received tradition" or prayers derived directly from the divine names in scripture, rather than later human inventions.</note> Prayers of ours, the highest divinity can best be celebrated and glorified—not indeed by fabricated and new titles, but by those most holy Names of **God** brought forth by the **Holy Spirit** Himself and described in the sacred pages:

<summary>This page explains that the various names and attributes of God in Scripture exist to help humans understand His infinite nature and goodness. It argues that using these divinely revealed names, such as the Tetragrammaton, is the most effective way to praise and love God through prayer.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader." The previous page discussed how the various names and titles of God in the Scriptures reveal His nature and virtues, leading the believer to praise. This page provides specific biblical examples of how the holy Name of God is used in prayer and blessing.</meta>

->AD LECTOREM.<-

# TO THE READER.

In a similar manner, Elizabeth, filled with the Holy Spirit, blessed Mary as she came to her, crying out with a loud voice: *Blessed art thou among women* (a manner of blessing which was taken from the books of **Judges** and **Judith**) *and blessed is the fruit of thy womb* (just as **Moses** had blessed the Israelite people). <note>See Luke 1:42. The author notes that these blessings aren't just spontaneous, but rooted in older scriptural traditions like Judges 5:24 or Deuteronomy 28:4.</note> Thus the Blessing with which **Christ** was greeted during his entry into Jerusalem was borrowed from the **Book of Psalms**. **Christ** also, in the agony of death while praying to the Father, did not disdain to express certain verses from the **Davidic Psalms** which contain the Names of **GOD**: as from Psalm 21, verse 1: *My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me?* <note>original: "DEus DEus meus quare me dereliquisti." This refers to the Vulgate Psalm 21, which is Psalm 22 in modern English Bibles.</note> Likewise from Psalm 30, verse 6: *Into thy hands I commend my spirit.* <note>original: "In manus tuas commendo Spiritum meum." This refers to Vulgate Psalm 30, which is Psalm 31:5 in modern English Bibles.</note>

For the Name of **God** cannot be better or more rightly glorified and sanctified unless that which is His is attributed to **GOD**: a thing which is usually done most fittingly through those verses that contain and explain His most holy Name. These things are to be rendered to **GOD**, and this repayment is truly called the "sanctification of the divine Name." 

And in this very respect, these prayers of ours differ from most others published and devised by the human brain: because in those [other] prayers, spiritual or physical goods are usually simply requested from **GOD**. However, in these prayers of ours, by the very fact that we implore **GOD's** help, we for the most part simultaneously glorify and celebrate the most holy Name of God—not indeed with simple words, [but] by reason of... 

<meta>catchword: huma- (likely continuing as "humana," referring to human reason or humanity)</meta>

<summary>This page explains that biblical figures like Elizabeth and Jesus himself used established scriptural names and verses to pray and bless. It argues that the author's collection of prayers is superior to others because it focuses on sanctifying God's Name rather than just asking for favors.</summary>
<keywords>Elizabeth, Mary, Jesus Christ, Moses, Psalms, sanctification of the Name, biblical precedent, theology of prayer</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader." The previous page concluded with the idea that our prayers are most effective when they use the specific names and verses provided by God in Scripture. This page elaborates on the theology of these Divine Names, comparing them to rays of light or stars that allow the human mind to perceive God's power without being blinded by His full glory.</meta>

->AD LECTOREM.<-

# TO THE READER.

...[not] by things humanly devised, but by those divine and most holy titles, made known to us by the **Holy Spirit** himself in the **Sacred Page** <note>The "Sacred Page" (sacra pagina) is a traditional term for the Holy Scriptures.</note>. For the Names of **GOD** are like images and rays of **GOD** himself, penetrating the souls of men through the celestial heroes <note>original: "cœlicolas Heroas." In the Renaissance and Neoplatonic tradition, this often refers to angels or exceptionally holy souls who mediate divine influence.</note>: whoever honors the Name of **GOD**, also honors **GOD** himself, as well as the rays, powers, and images of **GOD** hidden in the secret meaning of the names.

Therefore, we ought rightly to worship that which shines in **GOD**, as if it were the sun of the earthly sun. Since the eyes of our mind are quite unable to endure this immense light, we must turn them from here toward the **Sacred Scriptures**, as if toward the heavens. In these, the divine Names and epithets <note>original: "cognomenta." These are the descriptive titles or "surnames" applied to God in the Bible, such as "The Almighty" or "The Merciful."</note> have been divinely handed down, like stars suited to our eyes. From these stars, the specific virtues of **GOD**, his divine titles, and his praises flash out to us, just as the sun's power is reflected in the stars. For there is no doubt that we draw divine rays from this source and rightly praise the **Divine Sun**—the sacred fountain of such rays—as it pleases **GOD**. 

What is better than this Good? What is more blessed than this happiness? For from that burning focus on **GOD** and the heat of the mind, it happens that we meditate upon and practice all of God’s works, laws, and commands. We constantly read again whatever pertains to his worship, which later makes it easier for us to obtain even physical blessings. With such a skill of praying, the **Prophet Elijah** prayed to **GOD** that it might not rain, and it did not rain upon the earth for three years and six months. <note>This refers to the account in 1 Kings 17 and James 5:17, citing Elijah's prayer as an example of the power of focused, scriptural petition.</note>

<term>Holy Spirit</term> (**Spiritum sanctum**)
<term>Names of GOD</term> (**Nomina DEI**)
<term>Prophet Elijah</term> (**Elias Propheta**)
<term>Sacred Scriptures</term> (**sacras literas**)
<term>Divine virtues</term> (**diuina virtutes**)

<summary>The author explains that the Names of God act as "divine rays" or "stars" that allow humans to safely contemplate God's overwhelming light. He argues that meditating on these scriptural names leads to both spiritual fervor and physical blessings, citing the Prophet Elijah as an example.</summary>

->TO THE READER.<-

...and again with a similar prayer he moved him <note>The author is referring to the Prophet Elijah, who prayed for the heavens to be closed and then, as mentioned here, prayed again for rain to return (1 Kings 18).</note>, so that he gave rain from heaven. In this way, **Hezekiah**, the King of Judah, at the time of the siege of Jerusalem, did not arm an army; instead, he fortified himself and his people against the enemy with that very **Divine Artifice** <term>Divine Artifice (original: "artificium diuinum"): This refers to the specific, skillful method of prayer using the sacred Names and attributes of God to achieve a desired spiritual or physical result.</term> of prayer. Thus, with the enemies slaughtered and destroyed, he saved the city.

For this same **Hezekiah**, the aforementioned **Divine Artifice** was the reason that **GOD**, changing his decree, delivered him from sickness and from death itself, and prolonged his life <note>The author refers to the story in 2 Kings 20, where God grants Hezekiah fifteen additional years of life after a heartfelt prayer.</note>. We will be able to obtain similar and even greater things from **God** through that Artifice, since it is certain that true adoration—performed in faith and in the Name of **Jesus Christ**—is never done in vain. For the Son of **God** himself, by means of an oath, promised us a certain hearing: *Amen, Amen, I say to you, whatever you shall ask the Father in my name, he will give to you.* <note>original: "Amen Amen, dico vobis, quicquid petieritis Patrem in nomine meo, dabit vobis." (John 16:23)</note> Likewise: *Whatever you shall ask the Father in my Name, this I will do, so that the Father may be glorified in the son,* etc.

Furthermore, by that **Divine Artifice**, we obtain this dignity: it allows us to engage in conversation with **GOD**, and joins us to the choir and society of **Angels**. This happens when we perform the same duty as they do, attributing praise and glory to **GOD**. Whence the **Psalmist** <note>The "Psalmist" refers to King David, the traditional author of the Psalms.</note> says: *In the sight of the Angels I will sing psalms to you; I will worship toward your holy temple, and I will confess your Name,* etc. <note>original: "In conspectu Angelorum psallam tibi, adorabo ad templum sanctum tuum, & confitebor Nomini tuo." (Psalm 138:1-2)</note> But because among the duties of piety there is none more frequently commended in sacred literature than the true glorification of the **Divine Name**, there is no reason for us to linger longer here, especially

<meta>catchword: especially (original: sertim, the end of "praesertim")</meta>

<summary>This page argues that prayer using God's specific Names—the "Divine Artifice"—is a powerful tool that has historically saved cities, healed the dying, and connects the practitioner to the angelic choirs. It roots this practice in both Old Testament history (Hezekiah) and the New Testament promises of Jesus Christ.</summary>

<keywords>Divine Artifice, Hezekiah, power of prayer, Holy Names, Jesus Christ, Angelic Choir, Psalms, intercession</keywords>

->TO THE READER.<-

...joy, <note>The catchword from the previous page "gaudio" (joy) connects to the sense of the preceding paragraph, though the text here begins with "especially."</note> especially since we must discuss this matter more clearly elsewhere. For we have decided to write an introduction to **Celestial Magic** <term>Celestial Magic (original: "Magia coelestem"): A branch of Renaissance spiritual practice that sought to connect with the divine and the influences of the heavens through prayer, meditation, and the study of sacred names.</term>, or divine wisdom (which, however, for obvious reasons will hardly ever be published, but will only be shared with a few certain people worthy of such a **Mystery**) in which we will explain more extensively the true and proper use of such **Kabbalistic** <term>Kabbalistic (original: "Kabalisticarum"): Referring to a Christian adaptation of Jewish mystical traditions, focused on the secret meanings of the Hebrew alphabet and the Names of God.</term> prayers, and we will deal at greater length with the true sanctification of the **Divine Name** (which is the true foundation of true **Magic** <note>The OCR reads "Magnae," but the context of the work suggests "Magiae" (Magic).</note>, and its first step shown by **Christ** himself). There we will also add examples of **Magical blessings** and **Curses**, and we will also treat by what way and method a Person ought to worthily and justly prepare themselves to acquire the wisdom of **GOD**, and to obtain it from **GOD** together with other goods, both physical and spiritual. 

Once this wisdom is obtained, it will then be easier: to truly know **God** and the inner man (that is, the **Soul of Man**); to foresee the future; to understand **Mysteries** hidden in the sacred Utterances <note>original: "facris Eloquijs," referring to the Holy Scriptures.</note>; to investigate secrets; to be enriched with true riches and lasting wealth; to acquire true glory, a name, and perpetual memory; to heal the sick; to perform miracles; to receive visions and revelations; to truly love **God** and be loved by him; to be in an **ecstasy of mind** <term>Ecstasy of mind (original: "excessu mentis"): A state of spiritual rapture where the soul is thought to leave the body or transcend normal consciousness to experience the divine.</term>, that is, to be elevated or ascend to heaven, to be united or joined with **GOD** himself, and to be illuminated by the **Holy Spirit**; to behold the **Great Works** <note>original: "Magnalia"</note> of God; and also by the true and perfect...

# TO THE READER.

...to enjoy joy, and to accomplish other wondrous things with a safe conscience <note>original: "salua confcientia"; the author is reassuring the reader that these mystical practices are spiritually safe and do not constitute forbidden black magic.</note>, just as I, the **Author** and **Collector** of this little book, in this most holy study of **Divine Wisdom**, through unique revelations of the **Holy Spirit**, have attained such great **Mysteries**, which almost surpass human grasp, and are wonderful to see; more wonderful to hear: but by far most wonderful in feeling and intellect. May **Almighty GOD** grant, however, that these **Kabbalistic Prayers** <term>Kabbalistic Prayers (original: "Precationes Kabalifticæ"): Prayers based on the tradition of Christian Kabbalah, which used the Hebrew names of God and angelic hierarchies as a framework for Christian meditation and petition.</term> of ours, as well as the **Kabbalistic study** itself, and each and every thing concerning it, may lead to the perpetual praise and glory of His **Most Holy Name**, as well as to the salvation of all pious and **Christian Kabbalists** <term>Christian Kabbalists (original: "Chriftianorum Kabaliftarum"): Renaissance scholars who adapted Jewish mystical traditions (Kabbalah) to support Christian doctrines and seek a deeper connection with the Divine.</term>.

<note>The text transitions here from individual verses of blessing to a formal "Act of Thanksgiving" (Gratiarum actio). It draws on the "Great Hallel" (Psalm 136), known for its repetitive refrain of God’s eternal mercy, and the "Laudate" psalms (like Psalm 148), which call upon the entire cosmos—from the heavens to the angels—to join in praise.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a sequence of short scriptural blessings and introduces a formal Act of Thanksgiving. It emphasizes God's eternal mercy and the universal call for all creation, including the heavenly hosts, to offer praise.</summary>

# PRAYER FOR
### THE MERCY OF GOD,

### Joel: 2.

> *Turn back to the Lord your God, for he is gracious and merciful, patient and of great mercy.*

### Hebrews: 4.

> *Let us therefore approach the throne of grace with confidence, so that we may obtain mercy and find grace for help in our time of need.* <note>original: "opportunum auxilium" – literally "opportune help" or "timely assistance."</note>

This is Hebrews 4:16.</note>

# THANKSGIVING
### FOR THE MERCY
->of GOD.<-
### Isaiah 63.

# AN EXPOSITION
## OF THE LORD’S
->PRAYER.<-
### OUR FATHER.
### 2 Corinthians 6 <note>2 Corinthians 6:17-18.</note>
> *I will receive you, and I will be a father to you, and you shall be sons and daughters to me, says the Lord almighty.*

I bow my knees to the Father of our Lord JESUS CHRIST, from whom all fatherhood <note>original: "paternitas." In the context of Ephesians 3:15, this refers to the source of all family or lineage.</note> in heaven and

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a sequence of personal prayers drawn from Psalm 118 (119) and begins a formal theological exposition of the Lord's Prayer (the "Pater Noster"), opening with the paternal relationship between God and humanity.</summary>

a
Ephesians 3

# SUPPLICATION
### IN TRIBULA-
->tion.<- <note>The text uses "tribulation" to mean a state of great trouble, affliction, or suffering.</note>

### 2 Samuel 22 <note>Original: 2. Reg: 22. In the Latin Vulgate, the books of 1 and 2 Samuel were titled 1 and 2 Kings.</note>

> *In my tribulation I will call upon the Lord, and to my God I will cry out.*

# A PRAYER
### FOR PRESERVATION
-> against the plague. <-

### 1 Kings 8 and 2 Chronicles 6 <note>original: "3 Reg: 8. & 2. Paral. 6." In the Latin Vulgate, 1 Kings is titled 3 Kings (3 Regum). This passage comes from King Solomon's prayer at the dedication of the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>

> *If famine arises in the land, or pestilence, or corrupt air <note>original: "corruptus aer." This refers to the historical "miasma" theory, the belief that diseases like the plague were caused by "bad air" or environmental decay.</note>, etc. Whatever plague, whatever infirmity, every prayer and supplication that shall happen to any man and to all your people Israel: if anyone recognizes the plague of his own heart <note>original: "plagam cordis sui." A poetic way of describing personal suffering, guilt, or the internal "strike" of a disease.</note> and stretches out his hands in this house, you will hear in heaven, in your dwelling place, and you will be merciful again, and you will act, so that you may give to each according to all his ways, just as you see his heart.*

<term>Precatio</term> (Prayer); <term>pestis</term> (Plague); <term>Fames</term> (Famine); <term>pestilentia</term> (Pestilence); <term>Israel</term>; <term>Salomon</term> (Solomon).

<summary>This page concludes a series of comforting scriptural verses before introducing a specific "preservative" prayer against the plague. It cites the biblical account of Solomon, who asked God to hear the prayers of those suffering from famine or disease if they turned toward the Temple.</summary>

<keywords>pestilence, Solomon, King James Bible parallels, Temple dedication, miasma, plague of the heart, divine mercy</keywords>

<term>Psalms</term> — Ancient hymns and prayers from the Old Testament used here to provide a voice for the suffering soul.
<term>Death</term> — Represented here not just as a physical end, but as a source of "fear," "darkness," and "snares."
<term>Iniquity</term> — Moral wrongdoing or injustice that is described here as an overwhelming torrent or flood.
<term>Mercy</term> — The divine compassion (misericordia) that the speaker relies upon for restoration.
<term>Blessed is he who comes</term> — A traditional greeting of honor, often used in a Messianic context to welcome Christ.

<summary>This page weaves together several Psalm verses to express the soul's movement from the overwhelming terror of death and sin to a hopeful plea for divine life and salvation. It reflects a traditional prayer structure where the speaker identifies with the suffering of biblical figures before calling upon God's mercy.</summary>

### Tobit 12.

# EXPOSTULATION
<note>original: "EXPOSTVLATIO." In this context, an "expostulation" is a formal address or a pleading protest directed toward God, often seeking justice or protection.</note>
### AGAINST ENEMIES AND adversaries.

### Jeremiah: 1.

> They shall fight against you, and they shall not prevail, because I am with you, says the Lord, to deliver you.
<note>See Jeremiah 1:19.</note>

### Ecclesiasticus: 46.

> He called upon the Most High, the powerful one, in attacking the enemies on every side, and the great and holy God heard him, etc.
<note>original: "Eccl: 46." This refers to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 46:5, describing the exploits of Joshua.</note>

<term>wonders</term> <note>original: "mirabilia." Remarkable or supernatural acts of God.</note> <term>Israel</term> <note>The biblical nation or the collective people of God.</note> <term>EXPOSTULATION</term> <note>A prayer of complaint or a plea for intervention.</note> <term>Jeremiah</term> <note>A major Hebrew prophet.</note> <term>Ecclesiasticus</term> <note>Also known as the Wisdom of Sirach, a book of ethical teachings.</note> <term>enemies</term> <note>original: "inimicos." Those who oppose the practitioner or the divine will.</note>

# PETITION
## FOR VICTORY
### in war.

->Psalm 2<-

> Ask of me, and I will give you the nations as your inheritance. <note>original: "Postula à me & dabo tibi gentes hæreditatem tuam." In the context of this manuscript, this verse is applied as a promise of territorial and political victory granted by God.</note>

->1 Maccabees 3<-

> The victory of battle does not depend on the size of the army, but strength comes from heaven.

<margin>
a

# THANKSGIVING
## FOR VICTORY.

### 2 Chronicles 20 <note>The Latin text uses "2 Paral.", referring to Paralipomenon, the traditional name for the Books of Chronicles.</note>

> *Jehoshaphat, King of the Jews, having gained victory against the Moabites, Ammonites, and Edomites at <term>Ein Gedi</term> <note>A desert oasis located on the western shore of the Dead Sea.</note> in the valley, gave thanks with his whole army.*

### 1 Maccabees 4

> *And they all fled into the plain of the foreigners, and Judas <note>Referring to Judas Maccabeus, the Jewish guerrilla leader who led the revolt against the Seleucid Empire.</note> returned to the spoils of the camp, and they took much gold and silver, and <term>blue cloth</term> <note>original: "hyacinthum", likely referring to cloth dyed with a specific blue or violet pigment mentioned in the Bible.</note> and sea-purple, and great riches. And having turned back, they sang a hymn and blessed God in heaven.*

# PRAYER
## AND BLESSING
### KABBALISTIC COMPRISING SEVENTY-TWO VERSES
#### Of the Psalms by which the 72 most holy names of GOD are denoted.

<margin>
That prayer is described from Reuchlin: *On the Wonder-Working Word* <note>original: "Ex Reuchl: De Verbo mirifica". Johann Reuchlin was a famous 15th-century German humanist and the first commoner to master Hebrew, attempting to reconcile Jewish Kabbalah with Christian theology.</note>, book 2, chapter 19; however, it is without the testimonies of Holy Scripture, which the author of this little book has noted in the margin.
</margin>

### Joel 2 & Romans 10

> For everyone, whoever calls upon the name of the Lord, shall be saved. <note>A common refrain in both the Old and New Testaments regarding the power of the Divine Name.</note>

### Zechariah 13

> He will call on my Name, and I will hear him. <note>original: "Ipse vocabit Nomen meum, & ego exaudiam eum." Zechariah 13:9. This serves as the theological justification for the list that follows, emphasizing the reciprocal relationship between calling God's name and receiving an answer.</note>

> But I have hoped in you, O Lord; I said, "You are my God" **x** <note>original: "Ego autem in te speraui Domine, dixi DEVS meus es tu." Psalm 31:14 (Vulgate 30:15). A declaration of personal trust amidst abandonment or fear.</note>.

Psalm: 120
</margin>
> The Lord shall keep you; the Lord is your protection upon your right hand **y** <note>original: "Dominus custodiet te, Dominus protectio tua, super manum dexteram tuam." Psalm 121:5 (Vulgate 120:5). The "right hand" is traditionally the side of action and vulnerability in combat, where a shield-bearer would stand.</note>.

</margin>

Psalm 9:1 (Vulgate 9:2). The "wonders" (<term>mirabilia</term>) usually refer to God's acts of deliverance in history.</note>.

Kuph.

### SAYINGS CONCERNING PRAYER,
### AND DIVINE WORSHIP,
->collected from the Holy Bibles and the writings<-
->of the holy Fathers by<-
->I. S. C.<- <note>These initials likely refer to the compiler or editor of this collection, a common practice in early modern devotional books.</note>

->*Exodus 22.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring floral and vine motifs within a square border.</image-desc>If he cries out to me, I will hear him, for I am merciful <note>original: "Si clamauerit ad me, exaudiam eum, quia misericors sum." From Exodus 22:27. In the context of the Mosaic Law, this refers to God’s promise to hear the cry of the poor and the oppressed.</note>.

->*Deuteronomy 6. Matthew 4. &*<-
->*Luke 4.*<-

You shall worship the Lord your God, and him only shall you serve <note>original: "Dominum DEum tuum adorabis, & illi soli seruies." A fundamental command from Deuteronomy 6:13, famously cited by Jesus during his temptation in the wilderness to rebuff the devil.</note>.

->*Psalm 33.* <note>Modern Bibles: Psalm 34</note><-

The righteous cried out, and the Lord heard them, and from all <note>original: "Clamauerunt iusti, & Dominus exaudiuit eos, & ex omnibus..." From Psalm 34:17 (Vulgate 33:18). The sentence is completed on the following page: "...from all their troubles he delivered them."</note>

### Judith 4 <note>This verse is from the Book of Judith, which is found in the Catholic and Orthodox Bibles but usually placed in the Apocrypha in Protestant traditions. In this context, the high priest is encouraging the Israelites to remain faithful during a time of national threat.</note>

Know that the Lord will hear your prayers, if you continue steadfast in fasting and prayers in the sight of the Lord. <note>original: "Scitote, quoniam exaudiet Dominus preces vestras, si manentes permanseritis in ieiunijs & orationibus in conspectu Domini."</note>

### Proverbs 15

The Lord is far from the ungodly, and he will hear the prayers of the righteous. <note>original: "Longè est Dominus ab impijs, & orationes iustorum exaudiet."</note>

### Isaiah 55

Seek the Lord while he can be found; call upon him while he is near. <note>original: "Quærite Dominum, dum inueniri potest, inuocate eum dum propè est."</note>

### Isaiah 58

You shall cry out, and the Lord shall answer; you shall shout, and he will say: "Here I am." <note>original: "Clamabis, & Dominus respondebit; vociferaberis, & dicet: ecce ego."</note>

### Isaiah 65

And it shall be, before they cry out, <note>The text breaks here at the end of the page; the full verse in the Bible concludes with "I will answer."</note>

**Vocabulary:** Judith, Proverbs, Isaiah, Lord, prayers, <term>fasting</term> <note>original: "ieiunijs"</note>, prayers, the ungodly, the righteous.

->Ecclesiasticus 15<- <note>original: "Eccl: 15." Although the manuscript attributes this to Ecclesiasticus, the text "Sacrificia impiorum abominatur Dominus..." is actually found in Proverbs 15:8.</note>

> The Lord abominates the **sacrifices** of the wicked, but the **prayer** of the upright is pleasing to him. <note>original: "Sacrificia impiornm abominatur Dominus , Et oratio rectorum placet ei."</note>

->Ecclesiasticus 35<- <note>Also known as the Wisdom of Sirach.</note>

> He who worships God with delight will be accepted, and his **petition** will reach even to the clouds. The **prayer** of the one who humbles himself will pierce the clouds; and until it draws near, he will not be consoled; nor will he withdraw until the Most High looks upon him. <note>original: "Qui adorat DEum in oblectatione , suscipietur , & deprecatio illius usq; ad nubes propinquabit. Oratio humiliantis se nubes penetrabit , & donec propinquet , non consolabitur, & non discedet, donec altissimus aspiciat."</note>

->Tobit 12<- <note>original: "Tobiæ 12." The Book of Tobit is a narrative found in the Catholic and Orthodox biblical canons.</note>

> **Prayer** is good with **fasting** and **almsgiving**, more so than storing up treasures of gold. <note>original: "Bona est Oratio cum jeiunio & eleemosyna magis, quàm thesauros auri recondere."</note>

**Vocabulary:** Sacrifices, Prayer, Ecclesiasticus (Sirach), Tobit, fasting, almsgiving <note>original: "Sacrificia, Oratio, Ecclesiasticus, Tobias, jejunio, eleemosyna"</note>

<meta>catchword: Matthew</meta>

<summary>This page presents a series of biblical excerpts emphasizing that God rejects the rituals of the wicked but favors the sincere, humble prayers of the righteous, especially when accompanied by fasting and charity.</summary>

### *Luke 18.*

> It is necessary always to pray, and not to lose heart. <note>original: "Oportet semper orare, nec deficere." This is the opening verse of the Parable of the Persistent Widow, where Jesus teaches the importance of perseverance in prayer.</note>

### *John 4.*

> True worshipers shall worship the Father in Spirit and in truth. For the Father also seeks such as these, who worship Him. <note>original: "Veri adoratores adorabunt Patrem in Spiritu & veritate. Nam & Pater tales quærit, qui adorant eum." Taken from Jesus's dialogue with the Samaritan woman at the well, emphasizing internal devotion over physical location.</note>

### *John 15.*

> If you abide in me, and my words abide in you, whatever you wish, you shall ask, and it shall be done for you. <note>original: "Si manseritis in me, & verba mea in vobis manserint, quodcunque volueritis, petetis, & fiet vobis." A promise found within the discourse of the True Vine.</note>

### *John 16.*

> Amen I say to you. If you ask the Father anything in my name, He will give it to you. <note>original: "Amen dico vobis. Si quid petieritis Patrem in nomine meo, dabit vobis." This highlights the "name of Jesus" as the authority by which believers approach God.</note>

->*Paul: to the Romans: 8.*<-

> For what we should pray as we ought, we do not know, but the Spirit himself intercedes for us with groanings that cannot be expressed. <note>original: "Quid oremus sicut oportet, nescimus, sed ipse spiritus postulat pro nobis gemitibus inenarrabilibus." From Romans 8:26, this passage suggests that human weakness in prayer is compensated by the Holy Spirit.</note>

->*Paul: to the Romans: 10.*<-

> The same Lord of all is rich toward all who call upon him: For everyone who calls upon the name of the Lord shall be saved. <note>original: "Idem Dominus omnium diues in omnes qui inuocant illum: Omnis enim quicunq; inuocauerit nomen Domini, saluus erit." From Romans 10:12-13, emphasizing that the "wealth" of God's mercy is accessible to anyone who prays.</note>

->*2. Corinthians: 11.*<- <note>The manuscript cites chapter 11, but the following theological reflection refers to the "thorn in the flesh" episode found in 2 Corinthians 12:7-9.</note>

> Even if he was not heard according to his own will, yet he was most certainly heard for his own benefit. <note>original: "Etsi non exaudiebatur ad suam voluntatem, tamen certissimè exaudiebatur ad suam vtilitatem." This is a commentary on Paul's prayer to have his "thorn" removed; the author notes that while God denied the specific request, the refusal served Paul's greater spiritual good.</note>

->*Philippians: 4.*<-

> Be anxious for nothing, but <note>original: "Nihil solliciti sitis, sed" The opening of Philippians 4:6. The catchword "in" at the bottom of the page indicates the text continues: "in everything by prayer and supplication..."</note>

<summary>This page compiles key Pauline teachings on prayer, emphasizing the Holy Spirit's intercession, the universal promise of salvation to those who call on God, and the idea that God answers prayers according to a person's needs rather than their specific desires.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the series of Apostolic exhortations regarding the practice of prayer, specifically concluding the passage from Philippians 4 before moving through Colossians, 1 Thessalonians, and 1 Timothy. The catchword "dia," suggests the next page begins with a word such as "diaphana" or "constantia," or perhaps continues the word "verecundia" (modesty).</meta>

...in every prayer and supplication, with thanksgiving, let your petitions be made known before God. <note>original: "in omni oratione & obsecratione, cum gratiarum actione petitiones vestræ innotescant apud DEum." This completes the sentence from Philippians 4:6, which began on the previous page. It establishes the "Philippian formula" for prayer: combining specific requests with a general attitude of gratitude.</note>

->*Colossians 4*<-

Be persistent in prayer, being watchful in it with thanksgiving. <note>original: "Instatæ orationi, vigilantes in ea in gratiarum actione." From Colossians 4:2. The Latin *instatæ* (be urgent/persistent) suggests that prayer is a discipline requiring active effort and mental alertness.</note>

->*1 Thessalonians 5*<-

Always rejoice, pray without ceasing, in all things give thanks. For this is the will of God in Christ Jesus for all of you. <note>original: "Semper gaudete, sine intermissione orate, in omnibus gratias agite. Hæc est enim voluntas DEI in CHRISTO IESV in omnibus vobis." From 1 Thessalonians 5:16-18. The command to "pray without ceasing" was a central theme for early modern spiritual writers, often interpreted as maintaining a constant awareness of God’s presence.</note>

->*1 Timothy 2*<-

I desire therefore that men pray in every place, lifting up pure hands without anger and dispute. Likewise also women, in modest apparel, with modesty <note>original: "Volo ergo viros orare in omni loco, leuantes puras manus sine ira & disceptatione. Similiter & mulieres in habitu ornato cum verecun..." From 1 Timothy 2:8-9. The "lifting of pure hands" refers to the ancient gesture of prayer (*orans*), while "without anger" emphasizes that the petitioner’s moral state affects the efficacy of the prayer. The text breaks off mid-word at *verecundia* (modesty or shamefastness).</note>

<summary>This page lists several key New Testament mandates on prayer, emphasizing that it should be persistent, constant, and conducted with a spirit of gratitude and moral purity.</summary>
<keywords>St. Paul, Epistles, persistence in prayer, thanksgiving, 1 Thessalonians, 1 Timothy, liturgical conduct, modesty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the text from page 259, completing the passage from 1 Timothy 2 regarding the modest conduct of women in prayer, followed by a sequence of apostolic excerpts from the Epistles of James and Peter regarding the moral requirements and efficacy of prayer.</meta>

...modesty and sobriety, <note>original: "dia, & sobrietate". This completes the word *verecundia* (modesty) from the previous page, where only the first two syllables were printed.</note> adorning themselves, not with braided hair, or gold, or pearls, or costly attire, but as becomes women professing godliness through good works. <note>original: "non in tortis crinibus, aut auro, aut margaritis, vel veste pretiosa, sed quod decet mulieres promittentes pietatem per opera bona." From 1 Timothy 2:9-10. The author emphasizes that internal virtue and charitable actions are the proper "ornaments" for a person of faith, rather than material wealth.</note>

->*James 4*<-

You ask, and you do not receive, because you ask wrongly, so that you may consume it upon your pleasures. <note>original: "Petitis, & non accipitis, eò quod malè petatis, vt in voluptatibus vestris insumatis." From James 4:3. This warns against "wrongful" prayer motivated by selfish desires or luxury rather than spiritual necessity.</note>

->*James 5*<-

The persistent prayer of a righteous person carries much weight. <note>original: "Multum valet deprecatio iusti assidua." From James 5:16. Often translated as "The effectual fervent prayer of a righteous man availeth much."</note>

->*1 Peter 3*<-

The eyes of the Lord are upon the righteous, and His ears are open to their prayers. <note>original: "Oculi Domini super iustos, & aures eius ad deprecationem illorum." From 1 Peter 3:12. This image of God's "eyes" and "ears" suggests an active, attentive divine presence responding to human suffering or need.</note>

->*1 Peter 4*<-

Be watchful in your prayers. <note>original: "Vigilate in orationibus." From 1 Peter 4:7. The concept of "watching" or "vigilance" in prayer implies a state of mental and spiritual alertness, guarding against distraction.</note>

->*1 John 5*<-

> This is our confidence, <term>fiducia<note>In a legal or theological sense, *fiducia* implies a firm trust or a "boldness" in approaching a higher authority.</note></term> which we have toward GOD: that whatever we ask according to His will, He hears us. And if we know that He hears us, whatever we ask, we know that we have the petitions that we request from Him. <note>original: "Hæc est fiducia nostra... scimus, quod habemus petitiones quas postulamus ab eo." From 1 John 5:14–15. This passage assures the believer that prayer is not a shot in the dark, but an interaction based on divine promise.</note>

->*Revelation 4*<-

> Fear GOD, and give Him honor, because the hour of His judgment has come. And adore Him who made heaven and earth, the sea and the springs of water. <note>original: "Timete DEVM, & date illi honorem... Et adorate eum qui fecit cœlum & terram..." While the text cites Chapter 4, these words are found in Revelation 14:7. The author may be citing a different chapter division or using a summary of the liturgical scenes found throughout the early chapters of the Apocalypse.</note>

->*Revelation 16*<-

> Give praise to our GOD, all you His saints, and you who fear Him, both small and great. <note>original: "Laudem dicite DEO nostro omnes sancti eius... pusilli & magni." Though cited as Chapter 16, this verse appears in Revelation 19:5. It serves as a universal call to worship, regardless of one's social standing.</note>

<meta>This page continues a thematic collection of authorities on the subject of prayer. While the previous page focused on New Testament scripture (1 John and Revelation), this page transitions to the "Church Fathers"—influential early Christian theologians—to provide further commentary on the spiritual efficacy and the necessity of persistence in prayer. The catchword "Basil" indicates the next page will begin with an excerpt from St. Basil the Great.</meta>

->Origen: on Numbers, Homily 25.<-

> One saint is far more powerful through prayer than countless sinners are through fighting. <note>original: "Multò plus valet vnus sanctus orando, quàm peccata inumeri pugnando." Origen (c. 185–253 AD) argues that the spiritual intercession of a single holy person outweighs the physical or military efforts of a multitude of the ungodly.</note>

->Cyril: on John, book 3, chapter 12.<-

> We do not know how to pray or what to ask for as is fitting; therefore, he himself bestows upon us those things which lead to eternal salvation. <note>original: "Nescimus orare ac petere, vt decet, quare ipse nobis porrigit, quæ ad salutem æternam conducunt." Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444 AD) reflects on human weakness, suggesting that God provides what we truly need for our souls even when we cannot articulate it.</note>

->Cyprian: book 4, Epistle 4.<-

> Let us ask and we shall receive, even if there is delay and slowness in the receiving because we have gravely offended; let us knock, for it is opened to him who knocks. <note>original: "Petamus & accipiemus, etsi in accipiendo mora & tarditas fuerit, quoniam grauiter offendimus, pulsemus, quia & pulsanti aperitur." St. Cyprian of Carthage (c. 210–258 AD) encourages persistence in prayer, acknowledging that a delay in God's response may be a result of human failings, but the promise of the Gospel remains sure.</note>

<meta>This page continues the collection of theological authorities on the nature and practice of prayer. *Basil: in the Hexameron, Book 9.* <note>original: "Basil: in Hexam: lib. 9." St. Basil the Great (c. 330–379 AD) was a hugely influential Greek bishop; his *Hexameron* is a famous series of sermons on the six days of creation described in Genesis.</note>

The Psalm puts demons to flight and invites angels to provide aid for salvation; it is a shield against the terrors of the night, a rest from the day's tasks, and a safeguard for labors. It is an ornament for boys and young men, a comfort for the elderly, and the most fitting grace for women. It causes deserted cities to be inhabited and teaches sobriety. For beginners, it becomes the first element of learning; for those progressing, it is their growth; and for the perfect, it is the firm foundation of their resting place. <note>original: "Psalmus Dæmones fugat... perfectis cubile firmamentum." Basil illustrates how the Psalms serve as a universal spiritual tool, providing protection, education, and peace regardless of the believer's age or status.</note>

->*In the same place:*<- <note>original: "Ibidem." This indicates that the following quote is also attributed to Basil, likely from his shorter rules or ascetic writings regarding the practice of prayer.</note>

How can one ensure that their mind does not wander during prayer? If they are certain that they are standing before the eyes of the Lord. <note>original: "Quomodo obtinebit quis, ut in oratione sensus eius non vagetur? Si certus sit adsistere ante oculos Domini." This advice addresses the common human struggle of distraction, suggesting that a profound awareness of God's immediate presence is the cure for a wandering mind.</note>

Psalmody <note>The practice of singing or reciting the Psalms as a form of worship.</note> is a consolation for those who weep, a cure for those who grieve, and health for the sick; it is known to be the remedy for the soul and the refuge <note>original: *suffragium*; here meaning a source of aid or support.</note> for all miseries. For whoever is deprived of such a gift is shut out from every benefit of comfort.

The more we are weighed down by the heavy turmoil of worldly <note>original: *carnalium*; literally "fleshly," referring to the distractions of physical and secular life.</note> affairs, the more ardently we must persevere in prayer.

# Bede. <note>The Venerable Bede (c. 672–735 AD) was an English monk and scholar. Here he is cited regarding the spiritual dangers of "mechanical" prayer.</note>

There are some who, upon entering the Church, prolong the singing of Psalms or their prayer with many words, but their heart is elsewhere, and they themselves do not reflect on what they are saying. Praying indeed with their lips, but wandering elsewhere in their minds, they are deprived of all the fruit of prayer; they think their prayer is heard by God, though not even they who pour it out are listening to it. <note>Bede is criticizing "lip service," suggesting that if the speaker isn't paying attention to their own words, they cannot expect God to do so.</note>

**Solomon:** Do not speak anything rashly in the presence of GOD. <note>Original: "Ne temerè quid loquaris coram DEO." <margin>*Francis Petrarch, On the Solitary Life, Book 1*</margin> <note>Petrarch (1304–1374) was an Italian scholar and poet, often considered the "Father of Humanism."</note>

<meta>This page concludes the collection of moral maxims and philosophical observations regarding prayer and the divine. <header>The same.</header> <note>Referring to the previous author, Juvenal.</note>

> And prayers have the power to sway the Lord of the world. <note>original: "Et Dominum mundi flectere vota valent." While the header suggests Juvenal, this sentiment mirrors themes found in Ovid regarding the efficacy of sincere prayer.</note>

<margin>Brufonius, book 5, chapter 24; and Erasmus, book 3, Apophthegms.</margin> <note>Giacomo Brufoni (Brufonius) was an Italian scholar known for his collections of historical examples. Desiderius Erasmus was a famous Renaissance humanist; his *Apophthegmatum opus* is a collection of sayings from classical antiquity.</note>

Socrates held that nothing should be requested from the Gods except for "good things" in their simplest form. This stands in contrast to the common people, who pray for a wife with a large dowry, for riches, honors, a kingdom, or a long life—acting as if they were prescribing to the divinity <term>numini: the divine will or power</term> what it ought to do. But God knows best what is good for us, and what is not.
